a. Presenting the Energy Community                                                      b. Members
The Energy Community extends the European Union (EU) inter-                             The Treaty establishing the Energy Community was signed in
nal energy market to its neighbouring countries. The principle                          October 2005 in Athens. Following ratification by all Parties,
objectives of the Energy Community are to create a regulatory                           the Treaty entered into force on 1 July 2006. As of 1 November
and market framework which is capable of attracting invest-                             2025, the Parties to the Treaty are the European Union, and nine
ments for a stable and continuous energy supply. By signing the                         Contracting Parties, namely Albania, Bosnia and Herzegovina,
Energy Community Treaty, the Contracting Parties committed                              Georgia, Kosovo*,1 North Macedonia, Moldova, Montenegro,
to implementing key EU energy legislation within a fixed time-                          Serbia and Ukraine. Armenia, Norway and Türkiye are Observers
frame.                                                                                  under Article 96 of the Treaty.




c. Institutional setting
The Energy Community has its own institutional framework. The highest decision-making body is the Ministerial Council, which
meets once a year to establish key priorities and adopt new legislation. The Energy Community Secretariat, based in Vienna, is inde-
pendent and performs the day-to-day work of the Community. The Secretariat is responsible for reviewing the progress made by the
countries in transposing and implementing European energy law incorporated by the Energy Community Treaty.



1
 Throughout this Implementation Report, this designation is without prejudice to positions on status, and is in line with UNSCR 1244 and the ICJ Opinion on the
Kosovo* declaration of independence.

         Focus: the EU’s targeted
         financial support

Financial support for reforms in 2024–2027

The Growth Plans for the Western Balkans and Moldova, to-                                   areas including energy markets, decarbonisation, renewable en-
gether with the Ukraine Facility, financially support the region’s                          ergy deployment and energy efficiency, and are aligned with the
energy transitions until 2027, facilitating the implementation of                           legal commitments of the Contracting Parties under the Energy
reforms, including those guided by the Energy Community. The                                Community Treaty.
EU has provided financial facilities to eight Energy Community
countries, linking the disbursement of macro-financial aid to the                           From a broader perspective, these frameworks reflect distinct
implementation of targeted reforms. These reforms focus on                                  yet interconnected goals:



1. FOR THE WESTERN BALKANS (WB6) COUNTRIES:                                                 2. FOR UKRAINE:

The overarching goal of the New Growth Plan3 is to drive the                                The Ukraine Plan5 addresses the exceptional challenges posed
green and economic transition. In the energy and climate sec-                               by the Russian war of aggression, focusing not only on imme-
tors, the reform agendas for the WB6 countries align closely                                diate reconstruction but also on medium-term energy and cli-
with the Energy Community process and the EU accession ob-                                  mate reforms under the Energy Community Treaty, as required
jectives, supporting the region’s accelerated integration into the                          for EU accession. The Ukraine Facility supports Ukraine in mak-
EU’s energy market while advancing sustainability efforts.                                  ing its energy system greener, more resilient and better integrat-
                                                                                            ed with the European market. This is backed by EU funding that
Building on the reform process, the Commission approved the                                 mobilises investments in Ukraine’s energy sector, in line with the
reform agendas of Albania, Kosovo*, Montenegro, North Mace-                                 country’s National Energy and Climate Plan, which aims for cli-
donia and Serbia in October 2024,4 followed by the first regular                            mate neutrality by 2050.
release of funds from the Reform and Growth Facility for the
Western Balkans in July 2025.



3. FOR MOLDOVA:

The Growth Plan for Moldova (2025–2027)6 provides targeted support for the country’s energy transition and resilience. EU fund-
ing, delivered through the Reform and Growth Facility, is conditional on reforms in the electricity and gas markets, renewable energy
deployment, energy efficiency and measures to strengthen energy security. A two-year strategy agreed with the EU aims to reduce
dependence on Russian supplies and fully integrate Moldova into the EU energy market and with Ukraine, in line with Moldova’s
Energy Community obligations and EU accession objectives.

         ACQUIS ON COMPETITION

    The acquis on competition (Articles 18 and 19 of the Energy Community Treaty) rests on three pillars:
    1. The prohibition of anticompetitive agreements established by Article 101 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union (TFEU);
    2. The prohibition of abuse of a dominant position provided for in Article 102 of the TFEU; and
    3. The prohibition of State aid granted in violation of Article 107 of the TFEU.

                      REGULATORY AUTHORITY                                                                                      82%

Legal setup and independence                                                  Incomplete transposition of the EIP limits ERE’s competences.
                                                                              ERE has nevertheless taken steps to incorporate certain EIP
The Energy Regulatory Entity (ERE) is Albania’s independent                   provisions into its regulatory acts.
regulator for electricity, natural gas and selected renewable en-
ergy issues. ERE is free from political or commercial influence.              Activities in the reporting period
It is accountable to Parliament, which exercises oversight, but
does not control ERE’s budget. ERE’s board consists of five                   In 2024, ERE launched procedures for approval of the investment
members appointed by Parliament. All members are currently                    plan of the TSO, OST, for 2025–2027 and for market coupling
within their five-year mandates.                                              between Albania and Kosovo*–all of which were approved in

 Regulatory authority                                                                                                                                           0,60
 Competition authority                                                                                                                                           0,10
 State aid authority                                                                                                                                             0,10
 Statistical authority                                                                                                                                          0,20
* in the case of Albania, the "Large combustions plants and industrial emissions" indicator is omitted from the final result as the Contracting Party does not have any
plants in operation
